### PSALM TEXT (Hebrew, English, LXX, Phonetic)
## Psalm 76 Text

### Verse 1
**Hebrew:** לַמְנַצֵּ֥חַ בִּנְגִינֹ֑ת מִזְמ֖וֹר לְאָסָ֣ף שִֽׁיר׃
**English:** For the leader; with instrumental music.
A psalm of Asaph, a song.
**Phonetic:** lam-nats-TSĒ-aḥ bin-ghiy-NŌTH miz-MŌR lə-'ā-SĀF SHIYR

### Verse 2
**Hebrew:** נוֹדָ֣ע בִּיהוּדָ֣ה אֱלֹהִ֑ים בְּ֝יִשְׂרָאֵ֗ל גָּד֥וֹל שְׁמֽוֹ׃
**English:** God has made Himself known in Judah,
His name is great in Israel;
**Phonetic:** nō-DHĀʿ biy-hū-DHĀH 'e-lō-HIYM bə-yis-rā-'ĒL gā-DHŌL shə-MŌ

### Verse 3
**Hebrew:** וַיְהִ֣י בְשָׁלֵ֣ם סוּכּ֑וֹ וּמְע֖וֹנָת֣וֹ בְצִיּֽוֹן׃
**English:** Salem became His abode;
Zion, His den.
**Phonetic:** way-HIY və-shā-LĒM sū-KŌ ū-mə-ʿō-nā-THŌ və-tsiy-YŌN

### Verse 4
**Hebrew:** שָׁ֭מָּה שִׁבַּ֣ר רִשְׁפֵי־קָ֑שֶׁת מָגֵ֬ן וְחֶ֖רֶב וּמִלְחָמָ֣ה סֶֽלָה׃
**English:** There He broke the fiery arrows of the bow,
the shield and the sword of war. Selah.
**Phonetic:** SHĀM-māh shib-BAR rish-fēy-QĀ-sheth mā-GHĒN wə-ḤE-rev ū-mil-ḥā-MĀH SE-lāh

### Verse 5
**Hebrew:** נָ֭אוֹר אַתָּ֥ה אַדִּ֗יר מֵֽהַרְרֵי־טָֽרֶף׃
**English:** You were resplendent,
glorious, on the mountains of prey.
**Phonetic:** nā-'ŌR 'at-TĀH 'ad-DIYR mē-har-rēy-TĀ-ref

### Verse 6
**Hebrew:** אֶשְׁתּֽוֹלְל֨וּ ׀ אַבִּ֣ירֵי לֵ֭ב נָמ֣וּ שְׁנָתָ֑ם וְלֹֽא־מָצְא֖וּ כׇל־אַנְשֵׁי־חַ֣יִל יְדֵיהֶֽם׃
**English:** The stout-hearted were despoiled;
they were in a stupor;
the bravest of men could not lift a hand.
**Phonetic:** 'esh-tō-lə-LŪ 'ab-BIY-rēy LĒV nā-MŪ shə-nā-THĀM wə-lō'-mā-tsə-'Ū khol-'an-shēy-ḤA-yil yə-dhēy-HEM

### Verse 7
**Hebrew:** מִ֭גַּעֲרָ֣תְךָ אֱלֹהֵ֣י יַעֲקֹ֑ב נִ֝רְדָּ֗ם וְרֶ֣כֶב וָסֽוּס׃
**English:** At Your blast, O God of Jacob,
horse and chariot lay stunned.
**Phonetic:** mig-ga-ʿa-RĀ-thə-khā 'e-lō-HĒY ya-ʿa-QŌV nir-DĀM wə-RE-khev wā-SŪS

### Verse 8
**Hebrew:** אַתָּ֤ה ׀ נ֥וֹרָא אַ֗תָּה וּמִֽי־יַעֲמֹ֥ד לְפָנֶ֗יךָ מֵאָ֥ז אַפֶּֽךָ׃
**English:** O You! You are awesome!
Who can withstand You
when You are enraged?
**Phonetic:** 'at-TĀH NŌ-rā' 'AT-tāh ū-miy-ya-ʿa-MŌDH lə-fā-NEY-khā mē-'ĀZ 'ap-PE-khā

### Verse 9
**Hebrew:** מִ֭שָּׁמַיִם הִשְׁמַ֣עְתָּ דִּ֑ין אֶ֖רֶץ יָֽרְאָ֣ה וְשָׁקָֽטָה׃
**English:** In heaven You pronounced sentence;
the earth was numbed with fright
**Phonetic:** mish-shā-MA-yim hish-MAʿ-tā DIYN 'E-rets yā-rə-'ĀH wə-shā-QĀ-tāh

### Verse 10
**Hebrew:** בְּקוּם־לַמִּשְׁפָּ֥ט אֱלֹהִ֑ים לְהוֹשִׁ֖יעַ כׇּל־עַנְוֵי־אֶ֣רֶץ סֶֽלָה׃
**English:** as God rose to execute judgment,
to deliver all the lowly of the earth. Selah.
**Phonetic:** bə-qūm-lam-mish-PĀT 'e-lō-HIYM lə-hō-SHIY-aʿ kol-ʿan-wēy-'E-rets SE-lāh

### Verse 11
**Hebrew:** כִּֽי־חֲמַ֣ת אָדָ֣ם תּוֹדֶ֑ךָּ שְׁאֵרִ֖ית חֵמֹ֣ת תַּחְגֹּֽר׃
**English:** The fiercest of men shall acknowledge You,
when You gird on the last bit of fury.-a
**Phonetic:** kiy-ḥa-MATH 'ā-DHĀM tō-DHEKH-khā shə-'ē-RIYTH ḥē-MŌTH taḥ-GŌR

### Verse 12
**Hebrew:** נִ֥דְר֣וּ וְשַׁלְּמוּ֮ לַיהֹוָ֢ה אֱֽלֹהֵ֫יכֶ֥ם כׇּל־סְבִיבָ֑יו יֹבִ֥ילוּ שַׁ֝֗י לַמּוֹרָֽא׃
**English:** Make vows and pay them to the LORD your God;
all who are around Him shall bring tribute to the Awesome One.-a
**Phonetic:** nidh-RŪ wə-shal-lə-MŪ lay-hō-WĀH 'e-lō-hēy-KHEM kol-sə-viy-VĀYW yō-VIY-lū SHAY lam-mō-RĀ'

### Verse 13
**Hebrew:** יִ֭בְצֹר ר֣וּחַ נְגִידִ֑ים נ֝וֹרָ֗א לְמַלְכֵי־אָֽרֶץ׃ {פ}
**English:** He curbs the spirit of princes,
inspires awe in the kings of the earth.
**Phonetic:** yiv-TSŌR RŪ-aḥ nə-ghiy-DHIYM nō-RĀ' lə-mal-khēy-'Ā-rets


### STRUCTURAL OVERVIEW
**Central Reading:** Psalm 76 portrays Zion as the lair of a divine Lion-Warrior whose single rebuke (גערה) disarms war itself. God's victory at 'Salem' (peace) involves no human combatant, and the punchline recasts a local deliverance (Judah/Israel, vv.2-3) as a universal, repeatable pattern in which human fury becomes praise and the kings of the earth bring tribute (vv.11-13). The Zion song works as a liturgy of disarmament: shalom is achieved not by Israel's arms but by God's shattering of all arms, and the 'lowly of the earth' (v.10), not merely Israel, are its beneficiaries.
**Genre:** Song of Zion (cf. Pss 46, 48, 84, 87, 122) with Divine Warrior victory hymn + Völkerkampf (assault-of-nations) motif; Asaphite/Elohistic collection; closing liturgical imperative (vows/tribute) → hymnic-cultic coda
**Context:** Asaph collection (Pss 50, 73-83), Elohistic Psalter: אלהים dominant, יהוה only v.12. LXX superscription πρὸς τὸν Ἀσσύριον → early reading as Sennacherib's 701 BCE withdrawal (2 Kgs 19:35; Isa 37:36: army 'dead' by morning ~ 'slept their sleep' v.6). Motif is not tied to one event: nations-attack-Zion-and-are-repulsed (Völkerkampf) shared w/ Pss 46:9-10, 48:5-8; Isa 17:12-14, 29:5-8. Exodus substratum: 'horse and chariot' v.7 ↔ Exod 15:1, 21; גערה at sea (Ps 106:9, 18:16, 104:7; Nah 1:4) → Reed Sea combat relocated to Zion. Judg 4-5 echo: נרדם/תרדמה of Sisera, heaven fighting (Judg 5:20 ↔ v.9 'from heaven'). Judah // Israel + 'God of Jacob' (v.7): Asaphite northern traditions (Jacob/Joseph) recentred on Jerusalem, plausibly post-722 unification theology. ANE: divine warrior thunder-voice (Baal's qôl shaking earth, KTU 1.4 VII); lion-deities (Sekhmet, Ishtar/Anat) → YHWH as lion in thicket; רשפי קשת evokes Resheph, Canaanite plague/archer god (Ugaritic ršp; Phoenician 'Resheph of the arrow' ršp ḥṣ); cf. Hab 3:5, Deut 32:24 → Resheph demythologized/subjugated, his arrows broken. Cultic setting: pilgrimage festival at Zion (vows paid, tribute brought v.12); סֻכּוֹ ('his booth/lair') may hint at Sukkot.

**Structure:**
  - v.1: Superscription: לַמְנַצֵּחַ בִּנְגִינֹת, מזמור לאסף שיר
  - vv.2-4: God known and enthroned in Judah/Zion; there He shattered weapons and war
  - vv.5-7: Direct address: radiant Warrior; enemy heroes plundered, frozen in death-sleep by divine rebuke
  - vv.8-10: Awesome One as cosmic Judge: sentence from heaven, earth stilled, lowly of all earth saved
  - vv.11-13: Coda: human fury converted to praise; call to vows and tribute; God humbles princes, awes kings of earth
  - macro-shape: Concentric/escalating design

**Poetic Devices (with function):**
  - Leitwort / root repetition ירא (vv.8, 9, 12, 13): נוֹרָא v.8, v.13; יָרְאָה v.9; מוֹרָא v.12 (divine title 'the Awesome One', cf. Isa 8:12-13); Thematic spine: fear moves from earth (v.9) to cultic title (v.12) to kings (v.13). Awe replaces warfare as the governing relation between God and nations.
  - Inclusio / geographic expansion (v.2 ↔ v.13; vv.9-13): Judah/Israel (v.2) → Salem/Zion (v.3) → heaven (v.9) → 'all the lowly of the earth' (v.10) → 'kings of the earth' (v.13); ארץ ×3 (vv.9, 10, 13); Local deliverance universalized. God 'known' (נודע) in Judah becomes 'awesome' (נורא) to all kings.
  - Extended metaphor: God as lion (vv.3, 5, 6): סֻכּוֹ thicket/lair, מְעוֹנָתוֹ den, הַרְרֵי טָרֶף mountains of prey; enemies = prey, 'despoiled' (אשתוללו); Subverts serene-Zion expectation: Temple = predator's den. Inverts predator/prey (mighty warriors become the kill). Cf. Amos 1:2, 3:8; Hos 5:14; Jer 25:38.
  - Sound play šem / šām / šāmayim / hišmaʿtā (vv.2, 3, 4, 9, 12): שְׁמוֹ 'His name' v.2, שָׁמָּה 'there' v.4, שָּׁמַיִם 'heaven' v.9, הִשְׁמַעְתָּ 'you proclaimed' v.9; also שָׁלֵם v.3 / שִׁבַּר v.4 / שַׁלְּמוּ v.12; Binds Name theology, sacred place, and heavenly verdict. Salem (peace) ↔ šallemû (pay vows) = shalom answered by cultic payment.
  - Metathesis / near-homophony נָאוֹר ↔ נוֹרָא (vv.5, 6, 8, 13): 'Resplendent' v.5 echoes 'awesome' vv.8, 13; many emend v.5 to נורא. Also אַדִּיר (God) v.5 ↔ אַבִּירֵי לֵב (enemies) v.6; Divine radiance vs human might set side by side through sound. True 'mighty one' contrasted with self-styled mighty.
  - Climactic list / abstraction (v.4): רשפי קשת, מגן, חרב, ומלחמה: concrete weapons → abstract 'war'; God abolishes war as institution, not just an army. Parallels Ps 46:10; Isa 2:4; Hos 2:20.
  - Demythologization (Resheph) (v.4): רִשְׁפֵי־קָשֶׁת 'flames/Reshephs of the bow': archer-plague deity's arrows broken; Divine rival reduced to broken ordnance. Possible plague nuance (cf. 2 Kgs 19:35) is subordinated to YHWH.
  - Sleep-to-death imagery (semantic chain) (vv.6-7): נָמוּ שְׁנָתָם → נִרְדָּם (tardemah) → 'did not find their hands'; Euphemistic, ironic depiction of annihilation without battle. Heroes immobilized, not defeated in fight.
  - Emphatic pronoun anaphora / apostrophe (vv.5, 7, 8): אַתָּה v.5; אַתָּה נוֹרָא אַתָּה v.8 (framing), plus vocative אלהי יעקב v.7; Shifts from report to doxological encounter. Frames divine identity as the sole actor.
  - Person/voice shifts (vv.2-13): 3rd person (vv.2-4) → 2nd person address (vv.5-11) → imperative plural (v.12a) → 3rd person (vv.12b-13); Moves from proclamation to worship to summons. Audience expands from Israel to 'all around Him'.
  - Tense shift (perfect → imperfect) (vv.3-10 vs vv.11-13): שִׁבַּר, הִשְׁמַעְתָּ, וַיְהִי (event) vs תּוֹדֶךָּ, תַּחְגֹּר, יֹבִילוּ, יִבְצֹר (ongoing/future); Historical victory becomes timeless paradigm and eschatological expectation.
  - Paronomasia on חֵמָה (v.11): חֲמַת אָדָם / שְׁאֵרִית חֵמֹת; possible puns Edom (אדם) / Hamath (חמת); Human rage converted into praise and divine attire. Irony of reversal: enemy's fury ends as God's belt/trophy.
  - Agricultural metaphor (vintage) (v.13): יִבְצֹר 'cuts off/gathers grapes': princes' רוּחַ harvested; Final image of effortless divine reaping. Human רוח vs divine גערה (breath/blast).
  - Legal-military fusion (vv.9-10): Battle vocabulary (vv.4-7) recast as court: דִּין, מִשְׁפָּט, קוּם 'rise' (judge rising to pronounce); Warfare reinterpreted as just verdict. Violence is subordinated to salvation of ענוים.
  - Parallelism (synonymous + staircase-like expansion) (vv.2, 3, 6, 13): v.2 Judah//Israel, known//great name; v.3 Salem//Zion, lair//den; v.6 tricolon of incapacitation; v.13 princes//kings; Parallel pairs create sacred-geography equivalences (Salem = Zion; Judah = Israel). Tricola mark climaxes (vv.4, 6, 8).

**Analyst's Working Notes (ambiguities, interpretive challenges, raw leads):** Core insight: psalm about abolition of war, not about a battle. No fighting is narrated, no Israelite army appears, no human king is mentioned (divine kingship only). The only effective force is divine speech/breath (גערה v.7, השמעת דין v.9) vs human רוח (v.13) and חמה (v.11). LXX reads Salem as ἐν εἰρήνῃ ('in peace'): peace-city as site of weapon-breaking = programmatic pun. Irony chain: mighty-hearted (אבירי לב) plundered; men of valor lose their hands; predators on prey-mountains become prey; fury becomes praise; princes harvested like grapes. Tension: God dwells in Zion lair (v.3) yet judges from heaven (v.9) → Zion as earthly point of heavenly throne (cf. Ps 11:4; 1 Kgs 8). Judgment (משפט) defined by its purpose, salvation of ענוים (v.10): divine violence is justified only as protection of the lowly; cf. Ps 72:4, 12. Closing movement mirrors pilgrimage: vows (Israel) + tribute (nations) converge at Zion (Isa 2:2-4; 18:7; 60; Ps 68:30-32). Exodus layering: horse/chariot, rebuke, 'who can stand'. Zion becomes the new Reed Sea. Possible chiastic centre vv.7-8: rebuke → 'who can stand?' Selah placement suggests liturgical pause after event (v.4) and after universal salvation (v.10). Divine-name distribution: אלהים vv.2, 10; אלהי יעקב v.7; יהוה אלהיכם v.12 (only covenant name, placed in cultic imperative) → covenant name reserved for worship obligation. Compare Ps 75 (preceding Asaph psalm: God judges, cuts off horns of wicked, cup of wrath) for sequential reading 75→76: promised judgment → enacted judgment. Downstream: check whether v.13 נגידים/מלכי ארץ echoes Ps 2:2, 48:5; check Targum/rabbinic application (Sennacherib, eschatological Gog).

**Open Questions:**
  1. Do סֹךְ/סֻכָּה and מְעוֹנָה elsewhere (Jer 25:38; Job 38:40; Nah 2:12-13; Ps 10:9; Amos 3:4) confirm a lion-lair reading? Does סֻכּוֹ also evoke Sukkot/booth (cf. Ps 27:5) → festival setting?
  2. Does רִשְׁפֵי־קָשֶׁת allude to the deity Resheph (Ugaritic ršp; Hab 3:5; Deut 32:24; Job 5:7; Song 8:6)? How does breaking Resheph's arrows relate to plague traditions of 701 BCE?
  3. Text-critical: v.5 נָאוֹר vs נוֹרָא; הַרְרֵי טָרֶף vs LXX ὀρέων αἰωνίων (הררי עד). Which reading best fits the lion imagery and the ירא Leitwort?
  4. v.11 crux: meaning of 'wrath of man shall praise You' and 'gird on remainder of wraths'. Evaluate emendations (אֱדֹם / חֲמָת place names; LXX ἑορτάσει = חגג). Is girding a trophy-belt (cf. Isa 59:17; Ps 65:7, 93:1)?
  5. גְּעָרָה as combat-myth term (Pss 18:16, 104:7, 106:9; Nah 1:4; Isa 50:2): how does it link v.7's 'horse and chariot' to Exod 14-15 and Chaoskampf?
  6. Sleep/stupor vocabulary (נום, שנה, רדם; Judg 4:21; Jer 51:39, 57; Nah 3:18): does it signal death-sleep? Does it correlate with 2 Kgs 19:35?
  7. Hapax-like form אֶשְׁתּוֹלְלוּ (Aramaizing Hitpolel of שׁלל): morphology, dating implications, and irony of plunderers plundered (cf. Isa 10:6, 33:1).
  8. Scope of ענוי ארץ (v.10) and כָּל־סְבִיבָיו (v.12): Israel only, or nations? Compare Zeph 2:3; Isa 11:4; Pss 68:30, 96:8; Isa 18:7 (tribute to Zion).
  9. יִבְצֹר v.13: vintage imagery vs 'cut off/restrain'. Possible Bozrah wordplay (Isa 63:1)? Relation to רוּחַ as breath/pride.
  10. Relation to Pss 46 and 48 (shared weapon-breaking, kings' terror, Zion inviolability): literary dependence, shared cult tradition, or Asaphite adaptation? How does the Asaphite 'God of Jacob' epithet function in a Judah-Zion psalm?

### VERSE-LEVEL NOTES
**Verse 1:** LXX adds 'πρὸς τὸν Ἀσσύριον' (against the Assyrian) as a secondary superscription absent from MT — earliest historicizing interpretation connecting the psalm to Sennacherib's 701 BCE withdrawal before any surviving Hebrew textual witness does so. The double genre tag מִזְמ֖וֹר + שִֽׁיר is notable, and the root of לַמְנַצֵּחַ (נצח = to prevail/conquer) creates ironic resonance with a psalm whose entire content is God's own prevailing.
- לַמְנַצֵּ֥חַ: Root נצח: (1) 'to lead/conduct' music → standard translation 'choirmaster/director'; (2) 'to prevail/conquer' → latent martial meaning in a victory hymn. Same root as נֵצַח (eternity, permanence). Piel participle + ל = ongoing role. 55× in Psalter superscriptions; the choirmaster title thus carries dormant conquest vocabulary in a psalm celebrating divine conquest. Possible deliberate wordplay, or at minimum acoustic irony.
- בִּנְגִינֹ֑ת: Stringed instruments — cf. Pss 4, 6, 54, 55, 61, 67 superscriptions (all Davidic/Asaphite). Curious juxtaposition: delicate plucked strings as vehicle for a psalm whose content is God shattering iron weapons. נְגִינָה can also mean 'taunt-song/mocking ditty' (Job 30:9; Lam 3:14; Ps 69:13) — does the martial psalm mockingly play Resheph's defeat on fragile strings?
- מִזְמ֖וֹר לְאָסָ֣ף שִֽׁיר: Double genre designation: מִזְמוֹר (instrumental piece) + שִׁיר (song/vocal) — cf. Pss 66, 67, 68 where both appear together. Order here מזמור first, שיר second (reversed in e.g. Ps 48). לְאָסָף = 'of/for Asaph' — authorship, guild dedication, or liturgical designation? Asaph collection (Pss 50, 73-83) is Elohistic; this psalm fits consistently (אלהים throughout except v12). LXX omits שיר in body but adds the Assyrian note, showing active interpretation of the superscription as occasion-marker.
**Verse 2:** The ordering Judah // Israel places Judah first — unusual since 'Israel' typically precedes or encompasses Judah in earlier texts; the reversal reflects Jerusalem-centric perspective while including the northern name 'Israel,' suggesting post-722 reintegration theology. The Niphal נוֹדָ֣ע casts God as the disclosing agent, not Israel as discoverer, and inaugurates the psalm's programmatic Name-theology.
- נוֹדָ֣ע: Niphal perfect of ידע — reflexive/passive: 'has made himself known,' divine initiative in self-disclosure. Not 'they knew God.' Cf. Exod 6:3 (same Niphal — God reveals his name YHWH to Moses); Ezek 38:23 (God makes himself known to nations in eschatological battle). Connection to Name theology in next colon (שְׁמוֹ) is tight: knowing God = knowing his name. Niphal here creates passive subject position: Israel is the recipient, not the seeker.
- בִּיהוּדָ֣ה... בְּיִשְׂרָאֵ֗ל: Judah-first order: inverts typical convention (Israel = whole nation precedes in most texts; post-division Israel = north). Asaphite context, plausibly post-722 BCE: northern 'Israel' has been destroyed, but the name is preserved as parallel to Judah — theological reintegration of northern identity under Zion's God. 'God of Jacob' (v7) is similarly northern. The pairing enacts liturgical reunification: Judah's sanctuary, Israel's covenantal name.
- גָּד֥וֹל שְׁמֽוֹ: Predicate-subject inversion: 'great is his name' — emphasis on greatness. שֵׁם here is programmatic: whole psalm elaborates what God's name means. Sound web: שְׁמוֹ (v2) // שָׁלֵם (v3) // שָׁמָּה (v4) // שָׁמַיִם (v9) // שַׁלְּמוּ (v12) — ש-מ cluster threading psalm as acoustic architecture binding Name/Place/Heaven/Worship. The psalm is a meditation on the content of that Great Name revealed across these sonic refrains.
**Verse 3:** סֻכּוֹ is brilliantly and probably deliberately double: 'booth/shelter' (festival-tent; Sukkot allusion) and 'thicket/lion's lair' (Jer 25:38; Job 38:40), meaning Zion is simultaneously a cultic festival shelter AND a predator's ambush-cover. LXX reads שָׁלֵם not as the place name Salem but as ἐν εἰρήνη ('in peace') — a translation theologically richer than it appears wrong.
- בְשָׁלֵ֣ם: Deliberate ambiguity: (1) Salem — pre-Davidic city of Melchizedek (Gen 14:18; Ps 110:4), archaic and priestly name for Jerusalem, invoking Melchizedekian sacral antiquity; (2) LXX ἐν εἰρήνη = 'in peace' — reads שָׁלֵם as adjective/noun from שָׁלוֹם. This LXX reading is not error: the homophony Salem/šālôm is almost certainly intentional — God's lair/booth is located at/in 'Peace,' and the weapons shattered there (v4) produce that peace. שַׁלְּמוּ (v12, 'pay vows') continues the š-l-m cluster: shalom answered by cultic payment at Shalom.
- סֻכּ֑וֹ: Critical lexical crux. Two roots in play: (1) סֻכָּה = booth, hut, shelter → cultic Sukkot tent (Lev 23:34; Ps 27:5 'he hides me בְּסֻכֹּה בְּיוֹם רָעָה'; Neh 8:14-17) — God dwells in pilgrim festival tent at Zion, pointing to autumn festival liturgical setting; (2) סֵךְ/סֹךְ = thicket, lair — lion's hiding/ambush cover (Jer 25:38: 'YHWH has left his lair/סֻכּוֹ like a lion'; Job 38:40: lion crouching בַּסֻּכָּה). Both meanings inextricably in play: Temple = festival booth = predator's lair. Zion is a site of both pilgrimage celebration and lethal ambush.
- וּמְע֖וֹנָת֣וֹ: מָעוֹן = dwelling, but specifically lion's/predator's den in marked contexts: Nah 2:12-13 (Nineveh as lion's מְעוֹנָה); Ps 10:9 (wicked crouches in מְסֻכּוֹ like lion at prey); Amos 3:4; Job 38:40 (lion in lair). The pair סֻכּוֹ // מְעוֹנָתוֹ = thicket // den = complete lion-habitat vocabulary. Zion as God's hunting ground subverts every expectation of a peaceful sanctuary, transforming pilgrimage into entering a predator's territory.
**Verse 4:** רִשְׁפֵי קָשֶׁת names Resheph, the Canaanite archer-plague deity (Ugaritic ršp; Hab 3:5; Deut 32:24), whose arrows YHWH shatters — not just weapons but a rival god demoted to broken ordnance. The weapon list then escalates from concrete to abstract: flaming arrows → shield → sword → WAR ITSELF, making God the abolisher of warfare as a category, not merely the winner of a battle.
- רִשְׁפֵי־קָ֑שֶׁת: Double reading inextricable: (1) 'fiery bolts/flames of the bow' — רֶשֶׁף = lightning bolt, flame (Song 8:6 'flames of fire/rišpê 'ēš'; Job 5:7 'sparks fly upward'); (2) 'Reshephs of the bow' — Resheph = Canaanite archer-plague deity (Ugaritic ršp; Phoenician ršp ḥṣ = 'Resheph of the arrow'; identified with Nergal; Deut 32:24; Hab 3:5 'Resheph at his heels'). Here YHWH shatters Resheph's arrows = demythologization of divine rival, reduction of a god to broken equipment. Plague associations (Resheph = disease-bringer, ršp = pestilence) may connect to 2 Kgs 19:35 (185,000 Assyrians dead overnight) — the angel of death figure collapsing into Resheph imagery. LXX κράτος τόξου = 'power of the bow' — entirely erases the divine name.
- שָׁ֭מָּה שִׁבַּ֣ר: שָׁמָּה = 'there' — emphatic deictic pointer back to Zion/Salem of v3. Specificity: the cosmic victory happened at a particular cultic locus; the divine Lion's lair is also the weapons-forge and weapons-graveyard. שִׁבַּר = Piel perfect 'shattered' — intensive action, complete breaking (cf. Ps 46:10 same verb for weapon-breaking at Zion). Sound echo: שָׁמָּה (there) // שְׁמוֹ (name, v2) // שָׁמַיִם (heaven, v9) — the ש-מ acoustic weave.
- מָגֵ֬ן וְחֶ֖רֶב וּמִלְחָמָ֣ה: Tricolon of escalating abstraction: מָגֵן (shield — defensive) → חֶרֶב (sword — offensive) → מִלְחָמָה (war — abstract category/institution). Not merely an arsenal destroyed but war-as-phenomenon abolished. Parallel: Ps 46:10 (bow, spear, chariot burned); Isa 2:4 / Mic 4:3 (swords to plowshares); Hos 2:20 (covenant of peace with weapons abolished). The climax on מִלְחָמָה is the theological shock: God doesn't win a battle, he unmakes the category of battle. Selah punctuates this staggering abstraction.
**Verse 5:** נָאוֹר ('resplendent/illuminated,' Niphal of אור) is a near-hapax in this sense and is suspect — LXX θαυμαστῶς ('wondrously') and the ירא Leitwort pressure both favor emending to נוֹרָא ('awesome/fearful'). The phrase הַרְרֵי טָרֶף ('mountains of prey') is extraordinarily obscure — LXX's 'eternal mountains' (ὄρος αἰώνιος, suggesting Vorlage הַרְרֵי עַד) may preserve a variant, but MT's 'mountains of prey' is lectio difficilior and sustains the lion-lair imagery of v3.
- נָ֭אוֹר: Hapax crux: Niphal participle of אור = 'illuminated/resplendent' — unique in this sense (נְאוֹרֹות = luminaries, Gen 1:14, is a different form/context). Many commentators emend to נוֹרָא (Niphal of ירא, 'awesome') — fits ירא Leitwort (vv.8, 12, 13), matches LXX θαυμαστῶς, and is contextually more powerful in a warfare psalm. Retaining MT: divine theophanic light blazing from mountain lair is defensible (Deut 33:2; Hab 3:3-4 — God's radiance from Teman; Ezek 43:2 — glory like lightning). The ambiguity between divine light and divine terror may itself be the point: same phenomenon, two registers of response.
- אַדִּ֗יר: Majestic, mighty — cultic superlative. Crucial: Exod 15:10-11 uses אַדִּיר of YHWH in the Reed Sea victory song ('glorious in holiness... doing wonders'). Direct echo establishes Exodus-Zion typology: what God did at the sea is replicated here. Sound irony: אַדִּיר (God, v5) vs. אַבִּירֵי לֵב (enemies, v6) — near-homophony א-ד/ב-יר sets God's true majesty against the self-styled mighty; the phonemes are nearly identical, the power distance absolute.
- מֵֽהַרְרֵי־טָֽרֶף: Three competing readings: (1) 'from the mountains of prey' = locative/ablative, God descends from lion-mountain lair (v3 extension) with prey; (2) 'more than mountains of prey' = comparative מִן, God is more magnificent than predator mountains; (3) 'on the mountains of prey' = God blazing atop mountains where prey is taken. LXX ὄρος αἰώνιος ('eternal mountains') suggests Vorlage הַרְרֵי עַד — attested phrase (Gen 49:26; Deut 33:15; Hab 3:6 הַרְרֵי עַד). MT טָרֶף (prey/torn flesh) is harder, more striking, and directly continues the lion-lair imagery of v3 (מְעוֹנָתוֹ/סֻכּוֹ). Textual decision has major consequences for how far the lion metaphor extends.
**Verse 6:** אֶשְׁתּוֹלְלוּ is an Aramaizing Hitpolel/Ishtaphel of שׁלל — the irony of 'plunderers plundered' is encoded in the verb's own morphology. The closing idiom לֹא מָצְאוּ יְדֵיהֶם ('did not find their hands') is unique in the Hebrew Bible: not 'could not use their hands' but 'could not locate them,' a terrifying image of dissociation from one's own body as the final stage of divine paralysis.
- אֶשְׁתּֽוֹלְל֨וּ: Rare Aramaizing Ishtaphel (or Hitpolel) of שׁלל = 'to plunder.' Standard Hithpael would be הִשְׁתּוֹלְלוּ; the aleph-prefix form is Aramaic morphology, attested in late BH — possible late date indicator or learned stylistic choice. Semantic irony intrinsic to the form: warriors who came as plunderers (שׁלל) are themselves reflexively plundered/despoiled. LXX ταράσσω ('were disturbed') = misses the irony entirely. Cf. Isa 33:1: 'woe, you who despoil without having been despoiled' — same root, same reversal. The Aramaizing form may be deliberate elevation marking the uniqueness of the scene.
- אַבִּ֣ירֵי לֵ֭ב: Mighty of heart — not merely physical prowess but specifically internal fortitude, volitional courage. אַבִּיר = strong one (bulls: Ps 22:13; stallions: Jer 8:16; angels: Ps 78:25). The locus 'of heart' (לֵב) is telling: heart-strength was their distinctive quality; in v7 they 'cannot find their hands' — extremities paralyzed, inner courage mocked by outer impotence. Sound echo: אַדִּיר (God's true might, v5) // אַבִּירֵי (enemy's claimed might, v6) — one letter's difference (דלת vs. בית), infinite power gap.
- נָמ֣וּ שְׁנָתָ֑ם: They slept their sleep — possessive suffix intensifies the finality: their own, proper, permanent sleep. נוּם = light drowsiness/nodding (Ps 121:3-4: YHWH does NOT נוּם — antithesis). Escalating sequence: נָמוּ (dozed, v6) → נִרְדַּם (deep supernatural stupor, v7, root ירדם = tirdema: Gen 2:21, 15:12, 1 Sam 26:12, Job 4:13). Cf. Jer 51:39, 57: Babylon's warriors made to sleep permanently by YHWH's judgment — same death-sleep idiom. The euphemistic progression makes slaughter invisible: no battle, no blood, only sleep deepening to death.
- לֹֽא־מָצְא֖וּ... יְדֵיהֶֽם: Did not find their hands — unique idiom in Hebrew Bible. Closest parallel: 1 Sam 23:22 ('find' in sense of locate an object). Here the sought object = one's own hands = total bodily dissociation, paralysis rendering a warrior severed from his own weapons/body. כָּל אַנְשֵׁי חַיִל = 'ALL men of valor' — no exceptions. The idiom escalates beyond 'could not use their hands' into something more uncanny: the mighty are not merely defeated, they cannot locate themselves.
**Verse 7:** The CAUSE of v6's total destruction is revealed only now — a single גְּעָרָה (divine blast/rebuke) — and 'horse and chariot' (רֶכֶב וָסוּס) is unmistakable Exodus vocabulary (Exod 15:1, 21), relocating the Reed Sea victory to Zion without explanation. The epithet 'God of Jacob' is a northern/patriarchal designation that sits incongruously in a Zion-Jerusalem psalm, marking Asaphite ideology.
- מִ֭גַּעֲרָ֣תְךָ: Prepositional מִן + גְּעָרָה (rebuke/blast) + 2ms suffix = 'from your rebuke.' גְּעָרָה is a charged combat-myth term: Ps 104:7 (waters of chaos flee); Ps 106:9 (Reed Sea rebuked and dried); Ps 18:16 (foundations exposed by divine blast-rebuke); Isa 50:2 (sea dried by rebuke); Nah 1:4 (sea rebuked). Encompasses: speech act (rebuke), breath-weapon (blast), battle-cry (war shout). One word = totality of God's combat power. Devastating economy: the elaborate weapon-list of v4 (destroyed by God) is replaced by a single divine exhalation. Human weapons vs. divine breath — no contest.
- אֱלֹהֵ֣י יַעֲקֹ֑ב: God of Jacob — patriarchal/northern epithet, unexpected in a Zion-Jerusalem psalm. Jacob-epithet signals Asaphite/northern perspective: Pss 77:15, 78:5, 81:5; Amos 3:13; 6:8; Hos 12:3. The epithet links to the northern Asaphite tradition of Jacob/Joseph (vs. Davidic/Judah framework). Theologically: the wandering tribal deity of Jacob (without fixed sanctuary) now enthroned at Zion — portable deity becomes permanent. God of Jacob = God who appeared to patriarch in exile = God who now appears for Zion's defense. Possible vocative function (O God of Jacob!) after the apostrophe of vv.5-6.
- נִ֝רְדָּ֗ם וְרֶ֣כֶב וָסֽוּס: נִרְדַּם = Niphal of רדם; תַּרְדֵּמָה = supernaturally imposed deep sleep (Gen 2:21 Adam's sleep; 15:12 Abram's vision-sleep; 1 Sam 26:12 Saul's camp; Job 4:13). Here: death-stupor imposed by God's rebuke. וְרֶכֶב וָסוּס = 'horse and chariot' — explicit Exodus vocabulary: Exod 15:1 ('the horse and its rider he threw into the sea'); 15:21; Exod 14:23, 28; 15:19. The Reed Sea victory is HERE relocated to Zion, implicitly equating the two events without comment. LXX 'those riding the horse' makes the equestrian image concrete.
**Verse 8:** The double pronoun אַתָּה נוֹרָא אַתָּה — 'YOU, awesome, YOU' — creates a chiastic envelope around the divine epithet for maximum intensity; this is the psalm's pivot from third-person report to doxological encounter. The word אַפֶּךָ ('your nose/anger') physically grounds divine wrath in the nostrils — the same nostrils whose blast (נִשְׁמַת אַפּוֹ, Exod 15:8) split the sea, acoustically linking back to v7's גְּעָרָה.
- אַתָּ֤ה ׀ נ֥וֹרָא אַ֗תָּה: Double emphatic pronoun framing נוֹרָא: chiasm אתה | נורא | אתה — maximum syntactic emphasis. נוֹרָא = Niphal participle of ירא, 'the awesome/feared one' — second major occurrence of ירא Leitwort (after v5 if emended; definitive here). If v5 is emended נאור → נורא, the Leitwort appears vv.5, 8, 12, 13 as consistent spine. The sudden isolation of אַתָּה after narrative vv.2-7 marks shift from proclamation to doxological encounter — the congregation turns to face the deity directly. LXX σύ φοβερός εἰμί = loses the chiastic doubling.
- מִי־יַעֲמֹ֥ד לְפָנֶ֗יךָ: Rhetorical question — answer: no one. Cf. Nah 1:6: 'who can stand before his indignation?'; Mal 3:2: 'who can endure the day of his coming?'; Ps 130:3: 'if you mark iniquities, who can stand?' — liturgically recognizable impossible-question formula. עָמַד לִפְנֵי = to stand before a superior (courtroom/royal audience); legal register persists from v9's דִּין. Ironic: קוּם (v10) — the Judge RISES while no enemy can STAND — verbal reversal built on same root.
- מֵאָ֥ז אַפֶּֽךָ: מֵאָז = 'from of old/from that moment' — temporal depth; God's wrath has been unanswerable since primordial time. אַף = nose (literal organ) metonymically = anger (physiological image: flared nostrils = fury). Same physiological image as נִשְׁמַת אַפּוֹ (Exod 15:8: God's nostrils' blast split sea); גְּעָרָה (v7) = that same nostril-blast now in Zion context. The chain: God's nose (אף) → God's blast (גערה) → horse and chariot stunned (v7) → earth silenced (v9). Divine anger is literally respiratory, literally lethal.
**Verse 9:** The spatial venue expands dramatically from Zion's lair (v3) to 'from heaven' — the battle-verdict originates cosmically, not locally. The earth's personified response יָרְאָה וְשָׁקָטָה ('feared and was stilled') uses שָׁקַט, the standard post-war rest formula of Judges (3:11, 5:31), casting cosmic silence as the peace that follows total divine victory. Crucially, the 'battle' is reframed entirely as a legal act — God 'makes his verdict heard' (הִשְׁמַעְתָּ דִּין), not 'wages war.'
- מִ֭שָּׁמַיִם הִשְׁמַ֣עְתָּ דִּ֑ין: Sound play: מִשָּׁמַיִם (from heaven) // הִשְׁמַעְתָּ (you caused to hear) — both ש-מ clusters. Add שְׁמוֹ (v2) // שָׁלֵם (v3) // שָׁמָּה (v4) // שָׁמַיִם (v9) // שַׁלְּמוּ (v12): ש-מ weave binding Name/Place/Heaven/Vow as acoustic theology. הִשְׁמַעְתָּ = Hiphil causative of שמע: 'you caused to be heard' — verbal proclamation, not thunder-noise alone; a speech-act. דִּין = legal verdict/sentence (not 'justice' abstractly but a specific ruling). Paired with מִשְׁפָּט (v10) = complete legal doublet framing the entire cosmic event as courtroom verdict, not military campaign. The battle was a trial all along.
- יָֽרְאָ֣ה וְשָׁקָֽטָה: Sequential verbs: earth feared → earth fell silent. יָרְאָה connects to ירא Leitwort (vv.8, 9, 12, 13) — the earth itself joins the grammar of awe. שָׁקַט = technical post-battle rest formula in Judges: 'and the land had rest' (Judg 3:11, 30; 5:31; 8:28); שקטה ← deuteronomistic peace formula. Applied to the EARTH as subject: the whole cosmos quiets after the divine verdict. Earth is personified as character who hears, fears, and submits. Irony: earth is 'stilled' not by peace but by terror — שקט here is fear-induced silence, not tranquility.
**Verse 10:** The psalm's most surprising theological move: God rises for judgment — legal קוּם imagery — but the purpose is to SAVE כָּל עַנְוֵי אֶרֶץ, 'ALL the lowly of THE EARTH,' not just Israel. The beneficiaries are universalized and identified not by covenant but by social condition, creating a startling reversal: the psalm's violence against the powerful IS the salvation of the powerless, globally.
- בְּקוּם לַמִּשְׁפָּ֥ט: Infinitive construct ב + קוּם = 'in the rising of / when he rose.' Image of judge rising = pronouncing sentence (Isa 2:19, 21: 'when he rises to terrify the earth'; Isa 3:13: 'YHWH stands up to plead/judge'). Judge seated → stands to deliver verdict = legal convention. But this judge rises לְהוֹשִׁיעַ (to save) — shocking inversion: expected punitive judicial rising reversed into salvific rising. מִשְׁפָּט + דִּין (v9) = complete legal doublet; the whole cosmic event = a trial with a liberating verdict.
- כׇּל עַנְוֵי אֶרֶץ: כָּל = 'all' — unrestricted universalism. עַנְוֵי = plural of עָנָו (humble/meek/lowly), cognate with עֲנִיִּים but carrying nuance of those crushed structurally, not merely economically (Zeph 2:3; Isa 11:4; Ps 37:11). אֶרֶץ = earth (not אֶרֶץ יִשְׂרָאֵל). Triple ארץ: vv.9, 10, 13 — cosmic geography traced. Perfect antithesis: אַבִּירֵי לֵב (v6, mighty of heart) destroyed // עַנְוֵי אֶרֶץ (lowly of earth) saved. The Zion-victory is a global redistribution: power stripped from the great, protection extended to all the lowly. Israel is not named as the beneficiary here. Second Selah marks this as the structural climax.
**Verse 11:** The psalm's most cryptic verse: חֲמַת אָדָם תּוֹדֶךָּ ('the fury of man will praise/acknowledge you') is already paradoxical — involuntary doxology from enemy wrath — but שְׁאֵרִית חֵמֹת תַּחְגֹּר ('the remnant of fury you will gird on') is unprecedented: God donning leftover human rage as a trophy-belt. LXX reads תַּחְגֹּר as ἑορτάζω ('will keep festival'), suggesting a different verb (חגג not חגר) and an entirely different image.
- חֲמַ֣ת אָדָ֣ם תּוֹדֶ֑ךָּ: חֲמַת = construct of חֵמָה (heat, wrath, fury) + אָדָם (humankind/man). אָדָם is near-homophone of אֱדֹם (Edom) — some commentators (Dahood, Kirkpatrick) read place-name: 'the fury of Edom.' Similarly חֲמָת = city of Hamath (north Syria) — possible geopolitical reading. תּוֹדֶךָּ = Hiphil impf of ידה = 'will praise/confess/acknowledge you.' The verb of voluntary thanksgiving (תּוֹדָה psalms) applied here to compelled doxology from enemy rage: human wrath, designed to destroy God's people, becomes involuntary praise. Theological paradox: God's purposes are not thwarted; even enemy fury serves the doxological order. Cf. Gen 50:20 (evil intended → good accomplished).
- שְׁאֵרִ֖ית חֵמֹ֣ת תַּחְגֹּֽר: שְׁאֵרִית = remnant/remainder — eschatological-sounding term: the leftover of human fury after it turns to praise. חֵמֹת = plural of חֵמָה (multiple outbursts of wrath). תַּחְגֹּר = Qal impf of חגר = 'to gird on (a belt/sword).' The image: God girds on the remnant of human wrath as a belt/trophy — unprecedented in Hebrew Bible. Divine trophy-belt of enemy fury? War-spoil worn as adornment? Cf. Isa 59:17 (God girding righteousness as belt) — inversion here (girding enemy's rage). LXX ἑορτάζω = 'will celebrate festival,' implying Vorlage תָּחֹגֶּג (Qal of חגג = to keep festival) — completely different verb, image, theology. MT lectio difficilior and more startling; LXX may be interpretive smoothing.
**Verse 12:** This verse contains the psalm's sole occurrence of יהוה — singular in the otherwise entirely Elohistic psalm — suggesting either an unalterable liturgical formula, a deliberate climactic name-revelation, or a scribal lapse. The sudden plural imperative נִדְרוּ וְשַׁלְּמוּ breaks the narrative, conscripting the congregation into the cosmic event; and כָּל סְבִיבָיו ('all around him') may encompass surrounding nations, not only Israel.
- לַיהֹוָ֢ה: THE ONLY יהוה in the psalm — every other divine reference uses אֱלֹהִים (consistent with Elohistic Psalter's systematic substitution). Uniqueness makes it theologically explosive: (1) vow-formula 'to YHWH' (נדר ל + YHWH) may be an unalterable liturgical phrase (Deut 23:22: 'when you vow a vow to YHWH your God') resistant to Elohistic redaction; (2) deliberate climactic revelation: the Awesome One (מוֹרָא) is now named — the name withheld throughout the psalm of God's power is finally disclosed in the cultic moment; (3) scribal preservation of an archaic formula. The pairing with אֱלֹהֵיכֶם immediately after = 'YHWH your God' — full identification. שַׁלְּמוּ ↔ שָׁלֵם (v3): paying vows at the city of peace/Salem completes the ש-ל-מ arc.
- כׇּל־סְבִיבָ֑יו: All around him — deliberately ambiguous referent and scope: (1) all nations geographically surrounding Zion, drawn as pilgrims; (2) the full cultic assembly encircling the divine presence at the sanctuary; (3) cosmic assembly around the heavenly throne. The ambiguity is productive: the summons in v12 is issued to all who are around YHWH — potentially including nations. יֹבִילוּ שַׁי = 'let them bring tribute/gift' — שַׁי is rare (Isa 18:7: nations bringing tribute to Zion; Ps 68:30: kings bringing gifts). Isaianic parallel (Isa 18:7) is eschatological — nations voluntarily tribute-bearing at Zion.
- לַמּוֹרָֽא: To the Awesome One — מוֹרָא as substantivized form of ירא, functioning as divine title/epithet. Rare as freestanding divine name (cf. Deut 4:24; Isa 8:12-13: 'YHWH of hosts... let him be your מוֹרָא'). Leitwort chain: יָרְאָה (earth feared, v9) → מוֹרָא (Awesome One as title, v12) → נוֹרָא (v13) — fear-experience becomes cultic name becomes permanent attribute. The grammar of awe: event → epithet → ongoing condition. Receiving tribute under the title 'the Feared One' reframes votive offerings as protection-payments to an overwhelming sovereign.
**Verse 13:** יִבְצֹר ('he cuts off/harvests grapes') applies the vocabulary of vintage to princes' רוּחַ (spirit/breath/pride), creating the unsettling image of God as divine vintager effortlessly harvesting the life-breath of rulers — an agricultural rather than military conclusion. The psalm ends on its final word אָרֶץ ('earth'), completing the local-to-universal arc (Judah → earth) and closing not on Israel's joy but on universal kings' awe.
- יִ֭בְצֹר ר֣וּחַ נְגִידִ֑ים: יִבְצֹר from בצר: (1) 'to harvest grapes/cut clusters' (Deut 24:21; Judg 9:27; Isa 24:13; Lev 25:5) = primary vintage sense; (2) 'to restrain/withhold/cut off' (Gen 11:6; Job 22:25) = secondary sense. Applied to רוּחַ (spirit, breath, pride of princes): God harvests princes' vital breath like cutting grape-clusters — utterly effortless, agricultural rather than martial. Possible Bozrah (בָּצְרָה) wordplay: Edom's capital (Isa 63:1-3: God returning from Bozrah in blood-stained garments after treading nations like grapes in winepress) — same root בצר activating vintage-of-nations imagery. Contrast: God's own גְּעָרָה-breath (v7) stuns armies; human רוּחַ is passively harvested. Battle of breaths: divine exhalation (גערה) vs. human inhalation (רוח) — God wins.
- נ֝וֹרָ֗א לְמַלְכֵי אָֽרֶץ: נוֹרָא = final Leitwort occurrence — ירא root: יָרְאָה (earth feared, v9) → מוֹרָא (divine title, v12) → נוֹרָא (attribute to kings, v13). Inclusio: v2 נוֹדָע בִּיהוּדָה (known in Judah) ↔ v13 נוֹרָא לְמַלְכֵי אָרֶץ (awesome to earth's kings) — reputation grows from local acknowledgment to global terror. לְמַלְכֵי אָרֶץ: kings of the earth — final word אָרֶץ (third occurrence: vv.9, 10, 13) completes cosmic expansion. Triple ארץ traces the arc: earth-in-fear (v9) → all lowly of earth saved (v10) → all kings of earth in awe (v13). Psalm ends on global royalty in terror, not on Israelite congregation in worship — surprising, deliberate universalization of the final image.

**Open Questions:**
  1. Is סֻכּוֹ (v3) a deliberate double-entendre exploiting the identical form of 'festival booth' (Sukkot) and 'lion's thicket/lair' (Jer 25:38; Job 38:40) — and does this intentional ambiguity frame the Sukkot pilgrimage as entering a predator's territory rather than a peaceful sanctuary?
  2. Is רִשְׁפֵי קָשֶׁת (v4) an allusion to the Canaanite deity Resheph (Ugaritic ršp, Phoenician ršp ḥṣ = 'Resheph of the arrow'; Hab 3:5; Deut 32:24), and if so, does shattering Resheph's arrows constitute deliberate demythologization of a divine rival, reducing a god to broken ordnance under YHWH's feet?
  3. Why does the weapon-list in v4 escalate from concrete (fiery arrows, shield, sword) to the abstract category מִלְחָמָה ('war itself') — is this simply rhetorical climax, or does it constitute a claim that God abolishes war as an institution (cf. Ps 46:10; Isa 2:4; Hos 2:20), making Zion an eschatological disarmament zone?
  4. Is הַרְרֵי טָרֶף (v5, 'mountains of prey') the correct reading, or does LXX's ὄρος αἰώνιος point to an original הַרְרֵי עַד ('everlasting mountains'; cf. Gen 49:26; Hab 3:6) — and which reading better sustains the lion-lair metaphor that begins in v3 and runs through v6?
  5. What is the morphological and theological significance of the Aramaizing Ishtaphel/Hitpolel form אֶשְׁתּוֹלְלוּ (v6), which builds the irony of 'plunderers plundered' into the verb itself — is this a late feature suggesting post-exilic composition, or a learned stylistic choice marking the uniqueness of the ironic reversal?
  6. Why does 'God of Jacob' (אֱלֹהֵי יַעֲקֹב, v7) — a northern/patriarchal epithet common in Asaphite psalms — appear in a psalm centered on Zion/Jerusalem, and does its placement precisely at the moment of Exodus echo ('horse and chariot,' v7) signal a deliberate north-south theological synthesis: Jacob's God is the same one who acts at Zion as he once acted at the sea?
  7. What precisely does תַּחְגֹּר mean in v11 — God 'girding on' the remnant of human fury as a trophy-belt, a weapon, or divine adornment? And is LXX's ἑορτάζω ('will keep festival,' reading חגג not חגר) a theologically motivated softening of an incomprehensible image, or does it preserve an original that MT corrupted?
  8. Why does יהוה appear exactly ONCE in this otherwise entirely Elohistic psalm (v12) — is this an unalterable liturgical formula ('vow to YHWH') that resisted Elohistic redaction, a deliberate climactic disclosure of the divine name withheld throughout the psalm of divine power, or evidence of compositional complexity?
  9. Is יִבְצֹר (v13) primarily a restrain/cut-off verb or a vintage/harvest verb — and does the grape-harvest image activate the Bozrah wordplay of Isa 63:1-3, where God treads nations like grapes in a winepress, making the psalm's final image one of effortless divine vintage of human rulers rather than military defeat?
  10. Why does the psalm close (v13) on kings of the earth in terror rather than on Israelite worship or communal joy — and how does this universal closure interact with 'all the lowly of the earth' (v10) to create a complete social reversal: the global mighty are harvested/terrified while the global lowly are saved?
  11. How does the three-stage sleep sequence — נָמוּ שְׁנָתָם (they slept their sleep, v6) → נִרְדַּם (deep stupor, v7) — function: euphemistic death-imagery, deliberate echo of Sisera's lethal sleep (Judg 4-5, תַּרְדֵּמָה), or allusion to 2 Kgs 19:35 (Sennacherib's army 'all corpses' by morning, explaining LXX's Assyrian superscription)?

### נֵצַח [BDB Dictionary]
n.m. La 3:18 eminence, enduring, everlastingness, perpetuity (לנצח Ecclus 40:14 as 4 infr.);—נֵ֫צַח 1 S 15:29 + 3 times; נֶ֫צַח Am 1:11 + 36 times; sf. נִצְחִי La 3:18; pl. נְצָחִים Is 34:10;— eminence: נצח ישׂראל the Eminence of Israel (י׳) 1 S 15:29 (DrSam 98 glory of Israel); attribute of God 1 Ch 29:11 (‖ הוד, תפארת et al.). enduring of life, and so life itself as enduring: אָבַד נִצְחִי La 3:18 my endurance doth vanish (De sap of life, v. ii. נ׳).
